Examining Scriptural Literature

Critical Analyses of Biblical Textual Traditions, Theologies, and Historical Contexts

Unified Biblical Criticism

A Critical Examination of Scripture, History, and Tradition

Compiled and Edited by Alan Shaffer

October 2025

Author’s Note

Unified Biblical Criticism

October 2025

Table of Contents

Author’s Note

I spent twenty-six years as a Bible student. I didn’t set out to deconstruct anything. For most of my life, I believed the Bible was sacred, inspired, and beyond reproach. I devoted myself to its study and to defending it as the Word of God.

But in the process of writing this book—pulling sources together, comparing texts, studying archaeology, and weighing claims—I found myself facing questions I couldn’t ignore. What began as an attempt to confirm my faith became the path that dismantled it.

I don’t write as a professional scholar with a degree on the wall. I left high school early, earned my diploma later, and taught myself everything you’ll find in these pages by sheer persistence. What I lacked in formal credentials, I tried to make up for in years of patient reading, note-taking, and following the evidence wherever it led.

If this book feels dense, that is because I want to give the subject the seriousness it deserves. But if it also feels direct, that is because I have nothing to hide. My goal is not to attack the Bible, nor to defend it, but to let it be seen for what it truly is.

Whether you walk away from this book with your faith intact, reshaped, or left behind, I hope you gain a deeper respect for the Bible—not as a flawless monolith that dropped from heaven, but as a profoundly human record of struggle, belief, and meaning.

—Alan Shaffer

Crosby, MN

Structured Edition with TOC

Introduction / Purpose

Dedication

To those unafraid to ask where the text came from, the wisdom to examine evidence, and the integrity to follow honest inquiry—regardless of personal cost.

Epigraph

“Truth does not fear investigation. Truth has the strength to withstand scrutiny. Truth that cannot be scrutinized is not truth.”

“It is the mark of an educated mind to be able to entertain a thought without accepting it.”

— Aristotle

“I would rather have questions that can't be answered than answers that can't be questioned.”

— Richard Feynman

About the Author

Alan Shaffer is an independent researcher and writer who devoted twenty-six years to the study of the Bible. His journey began as a devout student seeking to understand scripture as divine truth, but through years of critical engagement, comparative study, and the process of writing this book, he deconstructed many of the assumptions he once held.

Without formal academic credentials, Alan’s authority comes from persistence and honesty. A high school dropout who later earned his diploma, he taught himself the tools of textual criticism, comparative mythology, and historical analysis by immersing himself in scholarly work and primary sources. His aim is not to mimic academia, but to make its insights accessible to the general reader without diluting their seriousness.

Alan lives in Crosby, Minnesota. His work reflects both the rigor of scholarship and the authenticity of a voice unbound by institutional constraints.

This volume undertakes a critical examination of the Hebrew Bible and its related scriptural traditions. The intention is not devotional but analytical: to approach these writings as literature, theology, and cultural artifact. Few texts have shaped human history more profoundly, and few are so often read without awareness of their complexity.

The approach throughout is grounded in three methodological commitments:

Critical honesty — the text is engaged without presuppositions of divine authority.

Comparative rigor — Israel’s scriptures are situated within the wider ancient Near Eastern world of Sumer, Akkad, Ugarit, and Egypt.

Transparency — scholarly debates are represented fairly, while the evidence is weighed directly.

Special attention is given to the plurality of textual witnesses—the Masoretic Text, Septuagint, Dead Sea Scrolls, and Samaritan Pentateuch—each of which preserves unique theological and ideological traditions. Likewise, the divine identity is explored in all its variation: YHWH, Elohim, El Elyon, and the god of the council.34

This book is written for readers who prefer inquiry to certainty, and who recognize that the power of these texts lies as much in their contradictions as in their continuities.

I am indebted to the generations of scholars who forged the field of critical biblical study. Julius Wellhausen’s source analysis of the Pentateuch remains foundational. Frank Moore Cross and Richard Elliott Friedman illuminated the textual and historical complexity of Israel’s scriptures. John J. Collins and Mark S. Smith continue to refine our understanding of Israel’s religion within its Near Eastern context.56789

SP — Samaritan Pentateuch

D — Deuteronomist (source behind Deuteronomy and Deuteronomistic history)

P — Priestly source (ritual, law, genealogies; exilic/post-exilic)

R — Redactor (editor/compiler of the Pentateuchal sources)

General Abbreviations

ANE — Ancient Near East / Ancient Near Eastern

cf. — compare (Latin confer)

et al. — and others (Latin et alii)

ibid. — in the same place (Latin ibidem)

id. — the same author (Latin idem)

s.v. — under the word (Latin sub verbo)

Scholarly References

BHS — Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia (standard critical edition of MT)

BHQ — Biblia Hebraica Quinta (updated critical edition in progress)

LCL — Loeb Classical Library

OT — Old Testament

Samaritan Pentateuch (SP) — Variants from the Samaritan recension are indicated where they diverge from MT and LXX.

Targumim — When cited, the Aramaic Targums are identified by abbreviation (e.g., Tg. Onq., Tg. Ps.-J.).

Rather than viewing textual variants as corruption, this study treats them as windows into the development of religious thought. The MT, LXX, DSS, and Samaritan Pentateuch represent different theological communities' approaches to the same underlying traditions. Their disagreements are not problems to be solved but evidence to be analyzed.

Key Example: The variations in divine names (YHWH vs. Elohim), genealogical numbers, and legal prescriptions across textual traditions reveal competing theological emphases rather than scribal errors.

2. Comparative Ancient Near Eastern Method

Scope and Sources

This study situates Israelite literature within its broader cultural context by systematic comparison with:

Mesopotamian traditions: Sumerian, Akkadian, and Neo-Babylonian texts

Ugaritic literature: Canaanite religious and mythological materials

Egyptian sources: Religious texts, wisdom literature, and historical records

Hittite materials: Treaties, legal codes, and religious practices

Criteria for Literary Dependence

The analysis distinguishes between three types of cross-cultural relationship:

Direct Literary Dependence: When Israelite texts show specific verbal or structural parallels that suggest direct borrowing (e.g., flood narratives, creation accounts)

Shared Cultural Background: When similarities reflect common ancient Near Eastern patterns without direct textual relationship (e.g., divine council imagery, kingship ideology)

Conscious Polemic: When Israelite texts deliberately invert or critique foreign traditions (e.g., Babel narrative vs. Mesopotamian temple ideology)

Methodological Safeguards

Against Maximalist Excess: Not every parallel indicates borrowing; some similarities arise from shared human concerns or regional cultural patterns.

Against Minimalist Denial: When parallels are extensive, specific, and follow sequential patterns, dependence is the most economical explanation.

Chronological Awareness: Priority is given to demonstrably earlier sources when establishing directions of influence.

3. Source-Critical and Redaction-Critical Analysis

Documentary Hypothesis Framework

While acknowledging ongoing scholarly debates about source criticism, this study employs a modified documentary hypothesis as a working model:

J (Yahwist): Southern, anthropomorphic portrayal of deity; emphasis on narrative and promise

E (Elohist): Northern, more transcendent divine presentation; prophetic emphasis

D (Deuteronomist): Centralization theology; covenant loyalty themes

P (Priestly): Ritual precision; genealogical framework; creation through divine speech

Redactional Analysis

The study pays particular attention to editorial processes that combined these sources:

Preservation of Tension: Editors often retained contradictory accounts rather than harmonizing them (e.g., dual creation narratives, multiple covenant traditions)

Ideological Shaping: Redactors arranged and modified materials to serve particular theological agendas

Historical Layering: Different historical circumstances produced different editorial emphases (pre-exilic vs. exilic vs. post-exilic)

4. Archaeological Integration

Scope and Application

Archaeological evidence is integrated where relevant to textual claims:

Settlement Patterns: To evaluate narratives of conquest, migration, and urbanization

Material Culture: To assess claims about religious practices, economic conditions, and technological development

Comparative Chronology: To establish synchronisms between biblical narratives and broader ancient Near Eastern history

Absence of Evidence: The archaeological silence regarding certain biblical claims is treated as significant data

Methodological Limitations

Correlation vs. Causation: Archaeological evidence can suggest context but rarely proves specific textual claims

Selective Preservation: The archaeological record is incomplete and potentially biased toward elite activities

This book begins from the conviction that scripture must be studied as a human product before it can be invoked as divine revelation. The writings we call “biblical” did not descend intact from heaven; they are the work of centuries of composition, redaction, and reinterpretation. They preserve theological conflict, ideological propaganda, mythological borrowing, and competing visions of God and humanity. To confront this head-on is not an act of hostility, but of intellectual honesty.

Textual Foundations

Four textual witnesses anchor this study: the Masoretic Text (MT), the Septuagint (LXX), the Dead Sea Scrolls (DSS), and the Samaritan Pentateuch (SP). Each is not merely a manuscript tradition but a theological stance, a claim about what counts as “Israel’s scripture.” The MT reflects a rabbinic consensus hardened in the centuries after the destruction of the Second Temple. The LXX embodies the diasporic experience of Jews in Hellenistic Egypt. The DSS preserve sectarian variants that diverge sharply from later canonical forms. The SP reveals a rival community, equally ancient, that read Israel’s story through the lens of Mount Gerizim rather than Jerusalem.1617

To treat the Bible as a single book is already to obscure this plurality. The text is not one voice but a chorus of competing witnesses.

Comparative Context

No text emerges in a vacuum. The literature of the Hebrew Bible belongs to the ancient Near Eastern world — a world populated by Sumerian flood narratives, Akkadian creation myths, Ugaritic divine councils, Egyptian hymns, and Canaanite rituals. The parallels are not incidental. The Genesis flood narrative is indebted to Atrahasis and Gilgamesh. Psalm 82 echoes Ugaritic conceptions of the divine assembly. Proverbs recycles Egyptian wisdom traditions.

If one refuses to acknowledge these parallels, one misreads the Hebrew Bible. Its genius lies not in originality but in adaptation — reworking shared myths into distinctive theological claims. To recognize this is not to belittle the text, but to see it more clearly.

Theological Tensions

At the heart of the Hebrew Bible lies a theological instability. God is called by many names: YHWH, Elohim, El Elyon, Shaddai, Adonai. At times these appear interchangeable; at other times they preserve older distinctions between deities that later redaction attempted to harmonize. Similarly, the text oscillates between monotheism, monolatry, and vestiges of polytheism. YHWH is portrayed as warrior, judge, father, husband, king — images that often conflict with one another.

The Bible is not a seamless revelation but a record of negotiation: tribes, kingdoms, priests, prophets, and exiles struggling to define their god and their identity. These tensions are not to be glossed over; they are the very evidence of the text’s human history.

Method

The approach in this work is deliberately devastating in its honesty but measured in its tone. The method rests on three pillars:

Critical honesty. No presumption of divine authorship or inerrancy. The Bible is treated as ancient literature, subject to the same scrutiny as Homer or Gilgamesh.

Comparative rigor. Every narrative is examined against its ancient Near Eastern environment. Where parallels exist, they are drawn explicitly.

The chapters that follow proceed sequentially through the Hebrew Bible’s formative narratives, from Genesis to the prophetic and post-exilic writings. Each section addresses not only the literary and theological content of the text but also its contradictions, moral implications, and historical plausibility. Special attention is given to passages often ignored in devotional reading but devastating in their implications: divine commands of genocide, failed prophecies, human sacrifice, and the silence of God.

The Samaritan Pentateuch (SP): The Torah as preserved by the Samaritan community, with distinctive readings that sometimes align with the LXX against the MT.

Key Terms and Concepts

Vorlage (German: "template"): The Hebrew manuscript that served as the source text for a translation or later copy. For example, the "LXX Vorlage" refers to the Hebrew text that the Greek translators were working from, which may have differed from our current MT.

Redactor: An editor who compiled, arranged, or modified earlier sources. Biblical texts often show signs of redactional activity—places where editors wove together different traditions or updated older material.

Qere/Ketiv (Hebrew: "read/written"): A system used by the Masoretes where the consonantal text preserves one reading (ketiv, "what is written") but the marginal notes instruct readers to pronounce a different word (qere, "what is read"). This often reflects theological sensitivities—for example, avoiding pronunciations of the divine name.

Critical Edition: A scholarly reconstruction of a text that presents the best available reading in the main text while noting significant variants in footnotes. The standard critical editions for Hebrew Bible study are:

BHS (Biblia Hebraica Stuttgartensia): The most widely used critical edition, based primarily on a single 11th century MT manuscript

BHQ (Biblia Hebraica Quinta): The newer critical edition that incorporates more manuscript evidence and DSS findings

How Textual Criticism Works: Basic Principles

Textual critics operate by several key principles when evaluating competing readings:

1. External Evidence: Age and Independence

Older manuscripts are generally preferred, as they are closer in time to the original. Independent manuscript traditions (MT, LXX, DSS) that agree with each other carry more weight than any single tradition alone.

Example: In Deuteronomy 32:8, the MT reads "according to the number of the sons of Israel," but both the LXX and a DSS manuscript (4QDeut^j) read "according to the number of the sons of God." The agreement of these independent early witnesses suggests the DSS/LXX reading is more original, with the MT representing a later theological revision.

2. Internal Evidence: Which Reading Best Explains the Others?

Critics ask: "Which reading would most likely have been changed into the other readings we see?"

Lectio difficilior potior ("the more difficult reading is stronger"): Scribes tend to smooth out difficult grammar or theology, so harder readings are often more original.

Theological tendency: Readings that support later theological developments are suspect. Scribes sometimes "improved" texts to align with their community's beliefs.

3. Transcriptional Probability

Understanding how scribes worked helps identify likely copying errors:

Haplography: Accidentally omitting repeated letters or words

Dittography: Accidentally repeating letters or words

Visual confusion: Mistaking similar Hebrew letters (ד/ר, ה/ח, etc.)

Harmonization: Making parallel passages match each other

Case Study: Goliath's Height (1 Samuel 17:4)

This example illustrates how textual criticism works in practice:

MT: "six cubits and a span" (approximately 9'9") LXX: "four cubits and a span" (approximately 6'9")

4QSam^a (DSS): Agrees with LXX

Analysis: The LXX and DSS agreement suggests Goliath was originally described as very tall but not superhuman. The MT's inflation to nearly 10 feet reflects a tendency to heighten the miraculous—making David's victory even more impressive. The principle of lectio difficilior supports the shorter reading: scribes would be more likely to make a giant bigger than to make him smaller.

The Challenge of Multiple Editions

Sometimes the evidence suggests that different versions aren't copying errors at all, but represent distinct editorial editions of the same work:

Jeremiah: The LXX version is about 15% shorter than the MT, with substantial rearrangements. Most scholars conclude these represent two different editions of the book, not just copying variations.

Samuel-Kings vs. Chronicles: These works often narrate the same events differently, reflecting different editorial perspectives and source materials.

Theological Implications

Textual criticism raises profound questions for communities that view scripture as authoritative:

If the "original text" is uncertain, which reading is authoritative?

When manuscripts disagree on theological matters (divine names, chronologies, genealogies), how do we choose?

What does it mean to claim scriptural inerrancy when the text itself is plural?

This study approaches these questions historically rather than theologically: we seek to understand how these texts developed and what their variations reveal about the communities that preserved them.

Reading Critical Apparatus

Modern critical editions display textual variants in footnotes called "critical apparatus." Here's how to read them:

1a MT G^L S: om G^S | ברא] + אלהים G^L

Translation: "Verse 1a is supported by MT, Lucian Greek manuscripts, and Syriac, but omitted in some Greek manuscripts. The word 'created' has a plus (addition) of 'God' in Lucian Greek manuscripts."

Why This Matters for Biblical Study

Understanding textual criticism is essential because:

It reveals the human history behind texts often treated as timeless

It exposes theological development by showing how communities edited texts over time

It provides tools for honest interpretation by acknowledging textual uncertainties

It demonstrates the complex relationship between "scripture" and "tradition"

Textual criticism does not destroy the authority of biblical texts—it reveals their rich, complex history. Rather than a single, unchanging "Word of God," we discover a collection of writings that have been treasured, interpreted, and carefully transmitted by communities across centuries.

The variants and differences are not problems to be solved but evidence to be understood. They show us how human communities have struggled to preserve and interpret their most sacred traditions. In the chapters that follow, we will see how this textual complexity illuminates the development of Israelite religion and the formation of the Hebrew Bible.

Canonization did not end the story of scripture; it began a new one. Once the Hebrew Bible was shaped into a recognizable form, it entered the living traditions of Judaism, Christianity, and Islam. Each community inherited the texts of ancient Israel, but none preserved them unchanged. Instead, every tradition reshaped, translated, and reinterpreted these writings into new configurations of authority.

After the destruction of the Second Temple in 70 CE, Judaism faced a crisis of identity. No longer bound to temple sacrifices, the Jewish people turned to scripture, interpretation, and synagogue ritual as their center. In this context, textual stability became paramount. Out of the plurality of Second Temple versions—the Dead Sea Scrolls, Septuagint Hebrew Vorlagen, Samaritan Pentateuch—one textual stream gradually prevailed.

This stream is the Masoretic Text (MT). Although “proto-Masoretic” manuscripts existed at Qumran, they were not yet exclusive. Only in the centuries after 70 CE did rabbinic authority consolidate this recension as normative. By choosing the MT, rabbinic Judaism defined its scripture not merely by content but by boundary: rival recensions, especially Samaritan Torah and Greek translations, were delegitimized as sectarian or foreign.

Between the sixth and tenth centuries CE, the Masoretes of Tiberias and Babylonia undertook the labor of final standardization. They inserted vowels (niqqud) into the consonantal Hebrew, added cantillation marks for synagogue chanting, and devised an intricate marginal apparatus (the masora) to guard against scribal deviation. Their work closed the door on textual fluidity.

The Masoretes did not simply preserve. They corrected. Where the consonantal text portrayed God in overly crude or anthropomorphic terms, they sometimes directed readers to pronounce alternative words (qere) rather than what was actually written (ketiv). In effect, they shaped how the Bible was to be read, even when the letters remained.

The Qur’an positions itself as both confirmation and correction of earlier scriptures. It affirms that the Torah and Gospel were divine revelations, yet it charges Jews and Christians with corrupting their texts through distortion (taḥrīf). In Qur’anic retelling, Abraham becomes the pure monotheist, neither Jew nor Christian. Moses confronts Pharaoh in ways resonant with Jewish midrash. Jesus is born of Mary through divine command, but is prophet, not God.

Judaism: The Masoretic Text—Hebrew, vowel-pointed, and jealously guarded as the authentic Torah.

The MT does not represent the Hebrew Bible as it existed in the time of the prophets or even the Second Temple period. It is the product of editorial decisions made roughly a millennium after much of the literature was composed. Earlier witnesses—such as the Dead Sea Scrolls—often differ dramatically, demonstrating that the Hebrew text was never fixed in antiquity.20

2. Expansions and Omissions.

When compared with earlier traditions, the MT frequently adds or subtracts material. The book of Jeremiah, for instance, is about one-seventh longer in the MT than in the Greek Septuagint. Deuteronomy in the MT shows harmonizations and theological adjustments that smooth over rough edges present in earlier forms. Such evidence reveals active redaction rather than simple copying.

By the time of the Masoretes, rival Hebrew textual traditions had been excluded. The versions preserved in the Dead Sea Scrolls, the Samaritan Pentateuch, and Greek translations represented alternate, equally ancient forms of Israel’s scriptures. These were sidelined or delegitimized, while the Masoretic recension was enforced as authoritative within Rabbinic Judaism and, through it, inherited by Christianity. The victory of the MT was not a matter of purity but of historical dominance.2223

Pentateuch Variants.

The Samaritan Pentateuch often agrees with the LXX against the MT, especially in Deuteronomy. This suggests that the LXX preserves a Hebrew textual tradition that was widespread but later suppressed by Rabbinic Judaism.

Messianic and Theological Renderings.

Samaritan-type manuscripts — texts aligning with the Samaritan Pentateuch.

Independent texts — forms of books like Jeremiah and Psalms that don’t align neatly with any later tradition.

The picture is devastating: in the Second Temple period, there was no single Bible—only a contested library of competing textual traditions.

Case Studies

Jeremiah.

The DSS confirm the LXX’s shorter version of Jeremiah. This means the MT’s expanded version is a later revision. The “Word of God” was literally edited, expanded, and rearranged.

Psalms.

Deuteronomy.

Some Qumran manuscripts harmonize or expand Deuteronomy, blurring distinctions between Pentateuchal sources. Others align with Samaritan traditions, showing that what later became “sectarian” was mainstream at Qumran’s time.

Genesis and Exodus.

Variants in genealogies, spelling, and even divine names demonstrate that scribes did not hesitate to alter sacred history. These changes were not “errors” but deliberate theological shaping.

Theological Devastation

The DSS destroy the illusion of textual uniformity. Instead of a divinely protected book, we find a battlefield of competing versions:

The Samaritan Pentateuch (SP)

If the Septuagint shows that the Hebrew text was already pluriform in the Hellenistic world, the Samaritan Pentateuch (SP) proves that rival Hebrew recensions—not just Greek translations—competed for authority. The SP is the Torah as preserved by the Samaritan community, with a base text that often differs from the later Masoretic tradition and, tellingly, sometimes agrees with the Hebrew Vorlagen behind the LXX.34

Provenance and Profile.

The SP crystallized within the community centered on Mount Gerizim (near ancient Shechem). Its distinctive features include:

Harmonizations across the Pentateuch. Repetitions are smoothed, parallel passages aligned, and apparent contradictions dissolved. This editorial habit signals late theological tidying—exactly the kind of redaction we also see, in different ways, in the MT.

Gerizim-centric readings. Most famous is the expanded Decalogue in Exodus 20/Deuteronomy 5, where a commandment enjoins building an altar on Mount Gerizim. Deuteronomy 27 likewise centers the cult there. These are not random “errors” but ideological re-centering of Israel’s worship away from Jerusalem.

Chronological and genealogical adjustments. Ages and totals in Genesis 5 and 11 sometimes track with non-MT traditions and with the LXX against the MT. That agreement signals a shared older Hebrew substratum that the MT later abandoned.

What SP Proves (and Destroys).

There was never a single, original, universally recognized Hebrew Torah in antiquity.

Competing priestly groups shaped “scripture” to legitimate their sanctuaries and political claims (Gerizim vs. Jerusalem).

The abstract survey of the Masoretic Text, Septuagint, Dead Sea Scrolls, and Samaritan Pentateuch establishes the plurality of witnesses. But it is in specific divergences that the myth of a single, unbroken, inerrant “Bible” collapses. What follows are a selection of the most striking case studies — examples that show not merely variant wording but entirely different theologies, laws, and histories preserved side by side in traditions that later communities treated as “Scripture.”3637

1. Jeremiah: Two Competing Prophets

2. Deuteronomy 27: Jerusalem vs. Gerizim

The Samaritan Pentateuch (SP) preserves a striking variant in Deuteronomy 27:4.

MT: “You shall set up these stones on Mount Ebal.”

SP (and a Dead Sea Scroll, 4QDeut^q): “You shall set up these stones on Mount Gerizim.”

Thus, the Torah itself testifies to the fracture lines of Israelite religion.

3. Isaiah 7:14 — Young Woman or Virgin?

One of the most consequential variants lies in Isaiah 7:14.

MT: “Behold, the young woman (עַלְמָה, almah) shall conceive.”

LXX: “Behold, the virgin (παρθένος, parthenos) shall conceive.”

5. The Case of Exodus and Numbers — Numbers Shift

In several places the MT and LXX diverge on the census and itinerary data of the wilderness wanderings. For example, the number of Israelites varies by tens of thousands between textual traditions. If these were divinely inspired statistics, how could they vary so drastically? What does “inerrancy” mean when God’s people supposedly numbered either 600,000 or far fewer, depending on which manuscript one reads?

These cases make the point unmistakable: the “Bible” has never been a single, stable text. It has always been plural — multiple Jeremiahs, multiple Torahs, multiple Psalters. To appeal to “the Word of God” is to choose, often unconsciously, one version among many competing scriptures.

Jeremiahs, Torahs, Psalters. The third pass drives the point further: not only do variants alter wording, but they shift theology, reshape history, and recast the divine itself. This is not a marginal phenomenon. It is the rule.

6. The Ten Commandments — Competing Moral Laws

The very core of Israel’s covenant exists in multiple, contradictory forms.

Exodus 20 grounds Sabbath in creation: “For in six days YHWH made heaven and earth, and rested on the seventh.”

Deuteronomy 5 grounds Sabbath in history: “You were a slave in Egypt, and YHWH brought you out… therefore he commanded you to rest.”

Samaritan Pentateuch inserts an eleventh commandment: to build an altar on Mount Gerizim, embedding Samaritan theology into the Decalogue itself.

9. Deuteronomy 32:8–9 — Polytheism Edited Out

One of the most revealing fractures comes in the Song of Moses.

MT: God assigns nations “according to the number of the sons of Israel.”

LXX and DSS (4QDeutᶥ): “according to the number of the sons of God (bene elohim).”

The difference is theological dynamite. The earlier reading reflects a divine council worldview: El Elyon apportions the nations among divine beings, with YHWH receiving Israel as his portion. The MT redactors erase the polytheistic background, domesticating the verse into Israelite monotheism.

The text itself bears witness to censorship.

10. Esther — Two Different Books

The “prophets” speak in different orders and lengths.

The “songs” were fluid, with psalms added and removed.

The “histories” preserve missing data and contradictory numbers.

The “God” of Israel appears in some versions as sole monarch, in others as one among many divine sons.

To read “the Bible” is already to make a choice — of which Bible. To speak of “the Word of God” is to speak of a word assembled from fractures, gaps, and redactions.

The inerrant, unbroken text never existed. What exists are rival witnesses, each reflecting a different community’s theology, each battling to suppress the others.

No aspect of biblical criticism generates more heat than the encounter between Genesis and modern science. For centuries, Genesis 1-11 was read as a scientific account of cosmic and biological origins. The rise of geology, astronomy, and evolutionary biology in the 18th-20th centuries shattered this reading, forcing interpreters into increasingly elaborate apologetics or outright denial of scientific evidence.

This chapter confronts the collision directly. Genesis was written by ancient Near Eastern authors who understood the cosmos in ways fundamentally incompatible with modern science. Recognizing this fact does not diminish the text's theological or literary power—but it does require intellectual honesty about what these chapters can and cannot tell us about the physical world.

The Ancient Near Eastern Cosmos: Genesis in Context

The Three-Tiered Universe

Genesis assumes a cosmos structured in three levels:

The Heavens Above: A solid dome (רקיע, raqia, "firmament") separating the upper waters from the inhabitable world below. The sun, moon, and stars are set "in" this dome (Gen 1:14-17), not as distant celestial bodies but as lights hung in a solid canopy.

The Earth: A flat disc floating on the cosmic waters, with Jerusalem/Israel at its center. Mountains serve as pillars supporting the heavens (Job 26:11; Ps 75:3).

The Waters Below: The cosmic deep (tehom) upon which the earth floats. Springs and rivers connect to this subterranean ocean, and in times of judgment, its "fountains" can burst forth (Gen 7:11).

This model, shared across Mesopotamian and Egyptian cosmologies, is scientifically obsolete. Yet Genesis presents it matter-of-factly, assuming its readers share this worldview.

The Firmament Problem

Genesis 1:6-8: God makes the firmament to separate waters above from waters below. This solid barrier holds back the cosmic ocean overhead—a necessary feature in a universe where rain comes from windows opened in the sky (Gen 7:11; 2 Kgs 7:2).

Modern reality: There is no solid dome above the earth. The atmosphere gradually thins into space. Rain forms through evaporation and condensation, not by opening celestial floodgates.

Chronology and the Age of the Earth

Biblical Chronology

Taking Genesis literally, various chronological systems emerge:

Genesis 1-2: Special Creation

Both Genesis creation accounts assume special creation—direct divine formation of species:

Genesis 1: God creates plants, sea creatures, land animals, and humans in distinct categories according to their "kinds" (min). Each category is stable and separate.

Genesis 2: God forms man from dust, then creates animals, and finally woman from man's rib. Direct, instantaneous creation is assumed throughout.

Theological implication: Humans are specially created in God's image, distinct from and superior to animals.

Evolutionary Biology

The evidence for common descent is overwhelming:

Fossil Record: Transitional forms demonstrate gradual change over millions of years

Comparative Anatomy: Homologous structures (vertebrate limbs, whale hip bones) reveal shared ancestry

Molecular Biology: DNA comparisons confirm evolutionary relationships; humans share 98.8% of DNA with chimpanzees

Observed Evolution: Bacterial resistance, finch beaks, peppered moths demonstrate natural selection in real time

Key point: Humans evolved from earlier primates over millions of years. There was no first couple named Adam and Eve.

The Theological Crisis

Evolution dismantles several core Genesis assumptions:

No special creation: Humans emerged through natural processes shared with all life

No first couple: Human evolution was gradual and population-based

Death before sin: Millions of species died and went extinct before humans existed

Animal kinship: Humans are animals, albeit highly sophisticated ones

Traditional doctrines of the Fall, original sin, and human uniqueness require radical reinterpretation or abandonment.

The Flood and Geology

The Biblical Claim

Genesis 6-9 describes a global flood covering "all the high mountains under heaven" (Gen 7:19). Every air-breathing creature dies except those in Noah's ark. The waters prevail for 150 days before receding.

Geological Evidence

If a global flood occurred within the last 10,000 years, it would have left unmistakable evidence:

Expected: A worldwide sediment layer deposited simultaneously

Reality: No such layer exists anywhere on Earth

Expected: Disruption of geological formations, ice cores, tree rings

Reality: Continuous sequences extend back hundreds of thousands of years

Expected: Fossil sorting by flood dynamics

Reality: Fossils are sorted by age, not density or swimming ability

Expected: Genetic bottlenecks in all species dating to the flood

Reality: Species show diverse genetic histories with no common bottleneck

Archaeological Silence

Civilizations contemporary with the supposed flood show no interruption:

Egypt: Continuous dynasties and cultural development through the 3rd millennium BCE

Mesopotamia: No break in Sumerian and Akkadian records

China and India: Unbroken cultural traditions spanning the alleged flood period

The silence is deafening. A global catastrophe killing all humans except eight people should register in human records—but it doesn't.

Local Flood Hypothesis

Some apologists retreat to "local flood" interpretations, but this creates new problems:

Textual contradiction: Genesis explicitly describes global coverage

Theological nullification: Why build an ark for a local flood when migration would suffice?

Narrative incoherence: The covenant with Noah and the rainbow sign lose their meaning if the flood was merely regional

Apologetic Strategies and Their Failures

Gap Theory

Proposal: Millions of years occurred between Genesis 1:1 and 1:2 ("without form and void" represents divine judgment on an earlier creation).

Problems:

No textual support in Hebrew

Contradicts Genesis 1's six-day sequence

Still can't accommodate evolution within the new creation

Day-Age Theory

Proposal: The "days" of Genesis 1 represent geological ages.

Hebrew yom with ordinal numbers (first day, second day) always means 24-hour periods

The sequence is wrong (plants before sun, birds before land animals)

Evening and morning make no sense for geological ages

Intelligent Design

Proposal: Accept an old earth but reject evolution; God intervened to create "irreducibly complex" systems.

No positive evidence for design

"Irreducible complexity" claims have been refuted case by case

Bad design (human eye, laryngeal nerve) suggests evolution, not perfect creation

Young Earth Creationism

Proposal: Accept Genesis literally; reject scientific dating methods and evolution.

Requires believing that virtually all of modern science is wrong

Cannot explain starlight from distant galaxies

Cannot account for geological formations, fossil distributions, or ice core data

Reduces God to a deceiver who created false evidence of age

Reading Genesis After Darwin

What Genesis Is

Genesis 1-11 are ancient cosmological myths that served their original audiences by:

Establishing Israel's God as supreme over other deities

Providing ethnic origin stories

Explaining the presence of evil and death

Legitimating Israel's covenant identity

These chapters are theology, not science. They address questions of meaning, purpose, and divine relationship—not the mechanics of cosmic and biological development.

What Genesis Is Not

Genesis 1-11 are not scientific accounts. They do not describe:

The actual age or structure of the universe

The biological history of Earth

Recognizing Genesis as myth does not destroy its value:

Literary artistry: The creation accounts are poetic masterpieces that have shaped human imagination for millennia.

Theological insight: Affirmations of human dignity, cosmic purpose, and moral responsibility transcend scientific details.

Rather than defending Genesis as science, we can ask different questions:

What theological truths do these stories convey?

How did ancient peoples understand their place in the cosmos?

What ethical implications follow from human emergence through evolution?

How might divine action work through natural processes?

Theological Reconstruction

Some possible directions:

Process Theology: God works through evolutionary development rather than against it

Emergence: Humans represent a qualitative leap in consciousness and moral responsibility, regardless of biological continuity

Mythic Truth: Genesis stories convey existential and relational truths that are independent of historical factuality

The collision between Genesis and modern science cannot be resolved through clever apologetics or selective denial of evidence. Ancient cosmology is simply wrong about the physical structure of the universe. Ancient chronology is wrong about the age of the earth. Ancient biology is wrong about the origin of species.

This does not make the biblical authors stupid or their work worthless. They wrote from within their historical context, using the best knowledge available to them. Their purpose was theological, not scientific.

A more courageous path acknowledges that Genesis reflects ancient understanding while preserving its theological and ethical insights. Such an approach requires abandoning the fiction of scientific inerrancy, but it opens space for authentic dialogue between religious tradition and scientific knowledge.

With the Creation Accounts —notably presented as two distinct narratives, there are two in Genesis

The first one In

Genesis 1 indicates a different order of events it pays out these events as follows

God creates by speaking Creation into existence in the span of 6 days, and Humans

(male and female) created together, last

Genesis 1: Creation Accounts, Elohim, and the Problem of Origins

The opening chapter of Genesis does not invoke YHWH at all. Instead, the deity who speaks the world into being is called Elohim — a grammatically plural term (“gods”) pressed into singular usage. Already, the supposed monotheistic foundation of the Hebrew Bible rests on a linguistic instability. The very first word used for the divine entity carries the memory of plurality.

The Priestly Structure and Theology

Whereas In

Genesis 2:

God created by hand with no timeframe mentioned

Man created first, then animals, then woman from man

God called “LORD God” (YHWH Elohim)

Focuses on Eden, man's role, and relationships.

This presents a difference in sequence and details between the two narratives in the fundamental process of creation

Genesis 1:1–2:4a is widely attributed to the Priestly source (P), a text shaped in the crucible of the Babylonian exile (6th century BCE). Its style is liturgical, structured around six days of creation followed by divine rest. Unlike mythologies of combat (such as the Babylonian Enuma Elish), this account suppresses any hint of divine struggle. Creation is by decree: Elohim speaks, and it is so.

This orderliness is often presented as theological sophistication — a “rational” deity above myth. But it is better understood as reactionary polemic: an exilic priesthood recasting Israel’s god as more exalted than Babylon’s Marduk, more transcendent than Canaan’s Baal, and free of the erotic chaos of older myths. The absence of struggle is itself ideological, not neutral.

Elohim, not YHWH

Nowhere in Genesis 1 is the divine name YHWH used. That figure only emerges in Genesis 2:4b, in the Yahwist narrative. The fact that Israel’s scripture preserves two distinct creation accounts back-to-back — one by Elohim, one by YHWH-Elohim — is devastating to claims of unity. The editors of Genesis did not smooth away contradiction; they canonized it.

This means the very first page of “the Bible” undermines any notion of a single, coherent voice. What later theology fuses into one God is, in the sources themselves, a polyphony of competing deities and traditions.

Foreshadowing El Elyon

El Elyon (“God Most High”) does not appear until Genesis 14, in the story of Melchizedek. Yet the choice to use the generic title Elohim here — rather than YHWH — already gestures toward a religious environment in which multiple divine names circulated. The “Most High” would later compete with YHWH for primacy, only to be absorbed into his identity. Thus, Genesis 1 is not the declaration of monotheism it is often claimed to be, but the opening salvo in a long process of theological consolidation.

Theological Contradictions in the Text Itself

Even within this first account, the cracks show. Light exists before the sun, moon, and stars. Earth exists “formless and void” before creation begins. Humanity is created “male and female” in a single act, in tension with the later account of Eve from Adam’s rib. Each contradiction reveals what happens when editors preserve variant traditions under the pretense of harmony.

Genesis 1 is not a singular revelation. It is a composite artifact of priestly ideology, linguistic ambiguity, and suppressed myth. And its choice of Elohim over YHWH leaves readers at the threshold of a fractured theology — one in which the identities of Elohim, YHWH, and El Elyon will clash and merge across the pages that follow.

Elohim, Suppressed Chaos, and the Battle of Myths

Elohim and the Problem of Plurality

The opening of Genesis employs the name Elohim, not YHWH. Grammatically, this word is plural (-im ending), literally “gods.” While later interpreters forced it into singular meaning through verb agreement, the residue of polytheism remains in the very grammar. Compare:

Elohim bara (“God created”) — a singular verb attached to a plural noun.

This is a linguistic scar: the text insists on singular action while its noun insists on multiplicity.

Later theology will claim this is a “majestic plural” or “plural of deliberation,” but such categories are absent from the Hebrew Bible itself. In other texts, elohim refers explicitly to divine councils or multiple gods (e.g., Ps. 82:1: “Elohim stands in the council of El; in the midst of the elohim he judges”). Thus, the opening word of scripture signals an unresolved tension: is this the one god of Israel, or a rebranded title for the gods of Canaanite myth?

Suppressed Chaoskampf

Genesis 1 seems antiseptic: Elohim speaks, and creation unfolds in orderly days. But beneath the surface lurks the ghost of combat myth. Verse 2 describes the pre-creation world as tohu va-bohu (“formless and void”), with darkness over the face of the deep (tehom).

Tehom is linguistically tied to Tiamat, the Babylonian sea-goddess slain by Marduk in the Enuma Elish. The Hebrew text strips away personhood, leaving only a watery “deep,” but the mythic resonance remains. Creation ex nihilo is not here; rather, Elohim imposes order on a primordial chaos, just as Marduk, Baal, and other storm gods did in rival traditions.

The silence about battle is not absence but erasure — a priestly attempt to overwrite polytheistic combat myth with the fiction of effortless divine speech.

Light Before Luminaries

On day one, Elohim creates light (or), yet the sun, moon, and stars do not appear until day four. This contradiction cannot be harmonized without absurdity. In ANE cosmology, celestial bodies were divine beings (Shamash the sun, Yarikh the moon). By creating light apart from luminaries, Genesis polemicizes against astral deities: light is no god, but an element at Elohim’s command.

This maneuver reveals the ideological project: de-divinizing nature. Genesis 1 demotes the sun, moon, and stars from deities to “lights” hung in the sky “to mark seasons and days.” What Mesopotamia revered as gods, Israel’s priests reclassified as cosmic furniture.

Humanity in the Image of Elohim

Genesis 1:26–27 declares: “Let us make humankind in our image, after our likeness.” The plural pronouns (“us,” “our”) cannot be explained away as rhetorical flourish. They reflect a background assumption of a divine council — a pantheon — in which Elohim is not alone. This is precisely the imagery seen in Ugaritic and Mesopotamian texts, where a high god addresses the assembly of gods.

The divine name YHWH is conspicuously absent. Genesis 1 is Elohim’s stage; YHWH enters only in Genesis 2:4b. This juxtaposition of Elohim-creation and YHWH-creation betrays multiple traditions spliced together by later editors. The existence of two contradictory creation accounts canonized side by side (Elohim in Genesis 1; YHWH-Elohim in Genesis 2) is not an accident but a window into the competition of deities.

Though El Elyon (“God Most High”) is absent here, the deployment of the generic Elohim hints at broader divine fluidity. In Canaanite religion, El was the head of the pantheon, father of the gods. In later Israelite tradition, YHWH will absorb titles like El Shaddai and El Elyon, collapsing multiple deities into one. Genesis 1’s refusal to use YHWH yet suggests it was written in an environment where the divine name was contested, and priestly editors opted for a title broad enough to subsume many claims.

Genesis 1 is not the pristine monotheistic revelation imagined by tradition. It is a polemical rewriting of older polytheistic myths, crafted in exile to exalt Israel’s god over rival deities. Its Elohim is a linguistic fossil of plurality; its creation is haunted by erased chaos-gods; its account of humanity assumes a divine council.

The very first page of the Hebrew Bible thus exposes the theological project: the suppression of polytheism under the fiction of unity, even as the traces of plurality remain visible to any reader willing to look.

Creation and the Illusion of Order

The opening chapter of Genesis is often read as the grand prologue of Scripture: a majestic declaration of cosmic beginnings, the triumph of divine order over chaos. Yet when examined critically, Genesis 1 unravels into a composite of older mythologies, priestly redaction, and cosmological assumptions long since abandoned by science.

The Priestly Framework and Babylonian Influence

The seven-day structure of Genesis 1 is not an eternal revelation but a liturgical construction, composed during or after the Babylonian Exile in the 6th century BCE. The Priestly authors, immersed in Mesopotamian culture, adapted Babylonian cosmological motifs and recast them into a weekly cycle culminating in the Sabbath. The “days” of creation parallel the ordered sequence of Enuma Elish, where Marduk establishes the calendar, stars, and cultic order after slaying Tiamat. Genesis 1 strips away divine combat but retains the scaffolding of cosmic scheduling, emphasizing ritual time rather than metaphysical origins.

The text assumes a cosmology far removed from modern science. The earth is conceived as a flat plane, supported above the waters below, with the raqia—a solid dome—holding back the cosmic waters above (Gen 1:6–7). The stars, moon, and sun are set “in the dome” as lights, not distant celestial bodies but luminaries fixed in a heavenly vault. Above the firmament lie the upper waters, a remnant of the ancient Near Eastern image of the heavens as a sea. Beneath lies the abyss, the tehom, directly cognate with Tiamat, the Babylonian goddess of the primordial deep. Far from transcendent revelation, Genesis 1 reproduces the obsolete science of its age.

Plurality of the Divine

Genesis 1:26 declares: “Let us make humankind in our image.” Apologetic readings that interpret this as a foreshadowing of the Christian Trinity are anachronistic. In its ancient context, the phrase reflects a polytheistic or at least plural conception of the divine. The “we” points either to a council of gods, as in Ugaritic and Babylonian parallels, or to YHWH/Elohim addressing a retinue of divine beings. The Hebrew Bible elsewhere preserves traces of this council (cf. Psalm 82; Job 1–2), making it clear that Israelite religion did not begin as radical monotheism but as a form of henotheism or monolatry. Genesis 1 thus bears witness to theological diversity that later traditions worked to suppress.

Conflict Between Sources

This priestly creation narrative was deliberately placed before the older Yahwist creation story of Genesis 2, which depicts YHWH forming Adam from dust and planting a garden. The juxtaposition is striking: Genesis 1 presents a transcendent Elohim creating by command, while Genesis 2 portrays YHWH as a hands-on artisan shaping clay and breathing life. The redactional decision to retain both accounts reveals that ancient editors did not regard contradiction as a problem. Instead, they preserved divergent traditions side by side, a fatal blow to claims of seamless harmony or divine dictation.

The Illusion of Creation ex Nihilo

Christian theology often claims Genesis 1 teaches creation “out of nothing” (ex nihilo). Yet the text itself presupposes primordial material: “the earth was formless and void, and darkness was upon the face of the deep (tehom).” The narrative begins not with nothingness but with a watery chaos over which the divine spirit hovers. The priestly authors never imagined absolute nothingness; they reworked earlier myths of chaos into an orderly liturgical framework. The doctrine of ex nihilo arises only centuries later, as theologians attempted to reconcile biblical cosmology with philosophical demands of divine omnipotence.

Theological Consequences

Genesis 1, far from presenting a pure revelation of timeless truth, exposes the fractures of Israelite religion. The Priestly editors sought to overwrite the anthropomorphic deity of older traditions with a transcendent liturgical god. Yet in doing so they imported Babylonian myth, encoded outdated cosmology, and left intact the marks of polytheism. What later readers take as a seamless “beginning” is in fact a palimpsest of competing worldviews—none of which align with modern conceptions of science or monotheism.

Genesis opens, not with certainty, but with contradiction. Its first words reveal a text born of exile, shaped by foreign influence, and riddled with traces of the very myths it claims to surpass. The so-called foundation of Scripture is itself fractured, its order an illusion constructed on chaos.

Transition: From Cosmic Order to Garden Narrative

The juxtaposition of Genesis 1 and Genesis 2 is not accidental but deliberate. By placing two distinct creation accounts back-to-back, the editors of the Pentateuch preserved theological plurality rather than resolving it.

Genesis 1 presents Elohim, the transcendent deity of priestly imagination, speaking creation into ordered existence, culminating in the sanctification of time through the Sabbath. Genesis 2 abruptly shifts tone and theology: here YHWH is no distant architect but an anthropomorphic craftsman, fashioning a man from clay, planting trees, and walking in the garden.

This tension between cosmic abstraction and earthy intimacy reveals the contested nature of Israelite theology. Elohim and YHWH are not simply different “names” for the same god; they embody divergent portrayals of the divine—one cosmic, one local, one universalizing, one tribal.

The redaction did not smooth over these differences. Instead, it set them side by side, leaving the reader to navigate contradiction as sacred. This decision testifies to a theological reality more complex—and more unstable—than later traditions admit.

The Garden of Eden narrative that follows (Genesis 2–3) will therefore not be read as a supplement or footnote to Genesis 1, but as an independent and competing tradition, preserving a vision of YHWH that stands in sharp contrast to the Elohim of priestly creation.

YHWH Introduced: The Tribal God in the Garden

With the shift from Genesis 1 to Genesis 2, a new divine figure steps onto the stage: YHWH Elohim. Unlike the remote Elohim of the priestly creation, YHWH is tactile, anthropomorphic, and local. He kneels in the dust, forms a man out of clay, and breathes life into his nostrils. This is not an abstract cosmic principle but a god who works with his hands.

The use of the compound title YHWH Elohim in Genesis 2–3 is striking. The tetragrammaton, YHWH, is the personal name of Israel’s god, historically tied to southern tribal cults and likely originating among the “Shasu of Yhw” attested in Egyptian texts of the Late Bronze Age. By coupling it with Elohim, the redactors appear to be merging two different traditions — one centered on Israel’s local deity, the other on the more generic Semitic word for “god.”

This anthropomorphic YHWH mirrors deities of the wider Ancient Near East. In Mesopotamian literature, gods like Enki (Sumerian) or Ea (Akkadian) fashion humanity from clay and walk among mortals. In Egyptian myth, Khnum forms humans on a potter’s wheel. The YHWH of Genesis 2 belongs to this same mythological register, a divine craftsman intimately bound to the soil and the land.

Such imagery challenges later theological claims of immutability and omnipresence. A god who shapes dirt, plants gardens, and strolls through the evening breeze is not the omnipotent abstraction of later philosophy, but a being whose presence is as physical as it is sacred.

Thus, when we enter the Garden of Eden, we do so not with the universal Elohim of Genesis 1, but with YHWH — the god of dust, garden, and breath. The story that unfolds is not one of cosmic order, but of intimate fragility, deception, and mortality.

YHWH and El Elyon: A Necessary Distinction

The Hebrew Bible preserves echoes of this structure. In Deuteronomy 32:8–9, the Dead Sea Scrolls and Septuagint preserve a version where El Elyon apportions the nations among the gods, while YHWH receives Israel as his own share:42

“When El Elyon divided up the nations,

when he apportioned the sons of man,

he fixed the boundaries of the peoples

according to the number of the sons of God.

But YHWH’s portion is his people,

Jacob his allotted heritage.”

This verse implies a polytheistic origin: El Elyon was not originally YHWH, but his superior, under whom YHWH functioned as Israel’s tribal deity.

By the time of Genesis 2–3, however, the redactors have begun to collapse these distinctions. YHWH Elohim fuses a local, anthropomorphic god with a more generic designation for divinity. Yet the memory of El Elyon as a distinct figure lingers in scattered texts.

Thus, when Genesis presents YHWH as the god of Eden, it is not a universal “Most High” ruling over all peoples, but Israel’s own god — later elevated to sole divinity. The Eden story, therefore, is best understood not as a cosmic myth of humanity under El Elyon, but as an Israelite tale of humanity’s beginnings under YHWH.

The Council of the Gods and the Politics of Eden

When the serpent has spoken, the fruit has been eaten, and humanity’s eyes are opened, the narrative does not end with a simple punishment. Instead, Genesis 3 closes with one of the most unsettling verses in the Hebrew Bible:

“Behold, the man has become like one of us, knowing good and evil. And now, lest he stretch out his hand and take also of the tree of life and eat, and live forever—” (Gen 3:22).

The phrase “like one of us” shatters the illusion of a solitary Creator-God presiding in isolation. The Eden story, like Genesis 1:26 (“Let us make humankind in our image, according to our likeness”), assumes the existence of a divine plurality. The text is not monotheistic in any strict sense; it reflects a world where Israel’s god, YHWH, is one figure among a broader divine assembly.

More Contradictions include

Genesis 6:3 says man's days shall be 120 years, yet: afterward Abraham lived 175 years (Genesis 12:4)

Sarah lived 127 years (Genesis 23:1)

Isaac lived 180 years (Genesis 35:28-29) Jacob lived 147 years (Genesis 47:28)

these contributions continue through the
